From each line strip the leading spaces and tabs; ignore the line when nothing is left or when what is left begins with `#`. Otherwise the address runs to the next space or tab or to the end of the line, one run of spaces and tabs is skipped, and the rest of the line, verbatim, is the cell's content. Exit 0 whenever the sheet parses.\n\nAn address is one or two letters naming a column and one to three digits naming a row, upper-cased when read, so `a1` and `A1` are one cell; columns run `A` to `Z` and then `AA` to `ZZ`, and the row is at least 1. Cells print in ascending column order and, within a column, ascending row order.\n\nContent that begins with `=` is a formula; anything else is a literal. A literal that is empty, or a cell nobody declared, is the number 0. A literal that matches an optional `-`, digits, and optionally a `.` and more digits is that number. A literal that is exactly one of the error tokens `#DIV/0!`, `#REF!`, `#VALUE!`, `#CYCLE!`, `#NAME?` and `#ERR!` is that error. Everything else is text.\n\nA formula body holds numbers written as digits with an optional `.` and more digits and no sign and no exponent, cell addresses, ranges written `A1:B3`, the bare words `TRUE` and `FALSE`, function calls, parentheses, and the operators `^`, `*`, `/`, `+`, `-`, `=`, `<>`, `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=`. Spaces and tabs may sit anywhere between tokens. Names are read case-insensitively. Precedence, loosest first, is the comparisons, then `+` and `-`, then `*` and `/`, then `^`, and then a leading `-` or `+`; the comparisons and the additive and multiplicative operators group to the left, `^` groups to the right, and a leading sign binds tighter than `^`, so `-2^2` is 4 and `2^3^2` is 512. Anything the grammar does not accept makes the cell `#ERR!`, and so does a range written anywhere but directly as a function argument, which the grammar does accept. A word the grammar accepts that is neither `TRUE`, `FALSE` nor the name of a call is read as a cell address, and one that is not one or two letters and one to three digits naming a row of at least 1 — `AAA1`, `A1000`, `A0` and `FOO` alike — is an address outside the addressable range rather than a grammar failure.\n\nThe functions are `SUM`, `MIN`, `MAX`, `COUNT` and `AVG`, each taking one or more arguments, `ABS` taking exactly one, and `IF` taking exactly three. A call with the wrong number of arguments is `#ERR!` and a call to any other name is `#NAME?`. An argument that is a range contributes every cell it covers, in ascending column and then ascending row order, whichever corner it was written from. `SUM` totals, `MIN` and `MAX` pick, `AVG` totals and divides by how many values it saw, and `COUNT` reports how many of its values are numbers. Every one of them but `COUNT` reads each value it takes as a number, so `ABS`, `SUM`, `MIN`, `MAX` and `AVG` take text as `#VALUE!` rather than ordering or ignoring it. `IF` reads its first argument as a condition, taking `TRUE` and a non-zero number as true and `FALSE` and zero as false, and returns only the branch it takes, so an error in the other branch is invisible.\n\nA value is a number, text, a boolean, or an error. In an arithmetic position a boolean is 1 or 0 and text is `#VALUE!`. Division by a zero divisor is `#DIV/0!`, and so is any arithmetic result that is not finite. A comparison compares two numbers numerically, two texts by code point, and two booleans with false below true; across kinds every number is below every text and every text is below every boolean. An address outside the addressable range is `#REF!`. A cell whose evaluation reaches itself, directly or through other cells, is `#CYCLE!`. An error reaching an operator or a function is the result: for an operator the left operand's error wins over the right one's, and for a function the first error among the arguments read left to right, ranges expanded, wins over everything else, including over `COUNT` counting.\n\n`values` prints `<address>=<value>` for every declared cell. * Recursive-descent parser for formula bodies. Produces an AST, or `null`\\n * when the text is not accepted by the grammar.\\n *\\n * Precedence, loosest first: comparisons, then + -, then * /, then ^\\n * (right-assoc), then a leading unary + or - (binds tighter than ^).\\n *\\n * A range (`A1:B3`) is only accepted directly as a function-call argument.\\n */\\n\\nimport { tokenize } from './lexer.js'\\n\\nconst FAIL = op, left, right }\\n  }\\n  return left\\n}\\n\\nfunction parseAdditive(ts) {\\n  let left = parseMultiplicative(ts)\\n  if (left === FAIL) return FAIL\\n  while (ts.peek().type === 'PLUS' || Precedence, loosest first, is the comparisons, then `+` and `-`, then `*` and `/`, then `^`, and then a leading `-` or `+`; the comparisons and the additive and multiplicative operators group to the left, `^` groups to the right, and a leading sign binds tighter than `^`, so `-2^2` is 4 and `2^3^2` is 512. is the program; how the work is split across other files under `src/` is up to you. It takes exactly one argument, `tokens` or `stats`, reads UTF-8 source text from standard input, and scans it. Exit 0 when the whole input scans.\n\nPositions count from 1. Reading a line feed moves to the next line and sets the column back to 1. Reading a tab moves the column forward to the next multiple of 4 plus 1, so a tab in column 1 lands on column 5 and a tab in column 6 lands on column 9. Every other code point moves the column forward by 1. A token's position is where its first code point sits.\n\nSpaces, tabs, carriage returns and line feeds separate tokens and produce none. `//` starts a comment that runs to the next line feed or to the end of the input. `/*` starts a block comment that ends at the matching `*/` and nests, so `/* a /* b */ c */` is one comment; a block comment that never closes is an error reported at the `/*` that opened the outermost one.\n\nAn identifier starts with a letter or `_` and continues with letters, digits and `_`; it is a `keyword` when it is exactly `let`, `if`, `else`, `while`, `return`, `true`, `false` or `null`, and an `ident` otherwise. A `number` starts with a digit. `0x` or `0X` introduces hexadecimal digits, `0b` or `0B` binary, and `0o` or `0O` octal; anything else is decimal digits, optionally a `.` and more digits, and optionally `e` or `E`, an optional `+` or `-`, and more digits. A `.` only continues a number when a digit follows it, and an exponent marker only continues one when a digit, or a sign and then a digit, follows it. A `_` may sit between two digits of the same run and nowhere else. A number followed immediately by a letter, a digit or `_` that the rules above did not consume, and a prefix with no digit after it, are both errors, and the reported text is everything the scanner ran through, up to and including those trailing characters.\n\nA `string` is written between double quotes and carries the escapes `\\n`, `\\t`, `\\r`, `\\\\`, `\\\"`, `\\0`, `\\xHH` with exactly two hexadecimal digits, and `\\u{H...}` with at least one hexadecimal digit naming a code point of at most 10FFFF. Any other escape is an error reported at its backslash. A line feed inside a string is an error reported where the line feed sits, and a string the input ends before closing is an error reported at its opening quote. A `rawstring` is written `r\"` then any characters, line feeds included, up to the first `\"`; it carries no escapes, its position is that of the `r`, and an input that ends before the closing `\"` is an `unterminated string` reported at that `r` rather than at the quote after it.\n\nThe punctuation, matched longest first, is `==`, `!=`, `<=`, `>=`, `&&`, `||`, `->`, `=>`, `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `%`, `=`, `<`, `>`, `!`, `(`, `)`, `{`, `}`, `[`, `]`, `,`, `;` and `.`. When two subsequences are equally long, take the one whose matches come earliest: walking both documents from the start, take a match whenever the two current lines are equal, and otherwise advance the side whose remaining subsequence is at least as long as the other's, preferring to advance the base when they tie. Every stretch the subsequence does not cover is a change, described as a run of base lines replaced by a run of that side's lines; an insertion replaces zero base lines and a deletion inserts zero lines.\n\nGather the changes from both sides into one list ordered by where they start in the base, with the changes from mine before the changes from theirs when they start at the same place. For a region several changes touch, each side's replacement runs from the smallest start of its own changes, moved back by however far the region begins before those changes do in the base, to the largest end of its own changes, moved forward by however far the region ends after them. Exit 0 when the merge is clean and 1 when any conflict was written, on every mode.\\n\\nCompare each side against the base with a longest common subsequence over whole lines, comparing them as exact strings. When two subsequences are equally long, take the one whose matches come earliest: walking both documents from the start, take a match whenever the two current lines are equal, and otherwise advance the side whose remaining subsequence is at least as long as the other's, preferring to advance the base when they tie. Every stretch the subsequence does not cover is a change, described as a run of base lines replaced by a run of that side's lines; an insertion replaces zero base lines and a deletion inserts zero lines.\\n\\nGather the changes from both sides into one list ordered by where they start in the base, with the changes from mine before the changes from theirs when they start at the same place. a tie.\\n *\\n * Returns a list of [sideIndex, baseIndex] pairs of matched (equal) lines,\\n * in increasing order.\\n */\\nfunction computeMatches(side, base) {\\n  const n = side.length\\n  const m = base.length\\n  // dp[i * (m+1) + j] = LCS length of side[i:] and base[j:]\\n  const dp = new Int32Array((n + 1) * (m + 1))\\n  const stride = m + 1\\n  for (let i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {\\n    for (let j = m - 1; j >= 0; j--) {\\n      if (side[i] === base[j]) {\\n        dp[i * stride + j] = dp[(i + 1) * stride + (j + 1)] + 1\\n      } else {\\n        const advanceSide = dp[(i + 1) * stride + j]\\n        const advanceBase = dp[i * stride + (j + 1)]\\n        dp[i * stride + j] = advanceSide > advanceBase ? advanceSide : advanceBase\\n      }\\n    }\\n  }\\n\\n  const matches = []\\n  let i = 0\\n  let j = 0\\n  while (i < n && j < m) {\\n    if (side[i] === base[j]) {\\n      matches.push([i, j])\\n      i++\\n      j++\\n    } else {\\n      const advanceBaseValue = dp[i * stride + (j + 1)]\\n      const advanceSideValue = dp[(i + 1) * stride + j]\\n      if (advanceBaseValue >= advanceSideValue) {\\n        j++\\n      } else {\\n        i++\\n      }\\n    }\\n  }\\n  return matches\\n}\\n\\n/**\\n * Turn matches between `side` and `base` into a list of changes: runs of\\n * base lines replaced by runs of side lines, covering every stretch the\\n * matched subsequence base.length]])\\n  for (const [i, j] of pairs) {\\n    const sideRun = side.slice(prevI, i)\\n    const baseStart = prevJ\\n    const baseEnd = j\\n    if (sideRun.length > 0 || baseEnd > baseStart) maxEnd = sorted.reduce((m, c) => Math.max(m, c.baseEnd), sorted[0].baseEnd)\\n\\n  const result = []\\n  result.push(...baseDoc.slice(region.baseStart, minStart))\\n  let cursor = minStart\\n  for clean side ('mine' | 'theirs' |\\n * 'both') with its lines, or a conflict with all three line sets.\\n */\\nfunction resolveRegion(baseDoc, region) {\\n  const mineChanges = region.touching.filter(c => c.source === 'mine')\\n  const theirsChanges = region.touching.filter(c => c.source === 'theirs')\\n  const baseLines = baseDoc.slice(region.baseStart, region.baseEnd)\\n  const mineLines = sideLinesForRegion(baseDoc, region, mineChanges)\\n  const theirsLines = sideLinesForRegion(baseDoc, region, theirsChanges)\\n\\n  if (arraysEqual(mineLines, theirsLines)) {\\n    return { type: 'both', lines: mineLines }\\n  }\\n  if (arraysEqual(mineLines, baseLines)) {\\n    return { type: 'theirs', lines: theirsLines }\\n  }\\n  if (arraysEqual(theirsLines, baseLines)) {\\n    return { type: 'mine', lines: mineLines }\\n  }\\n  return { type: 'conflict', mineLines, baseLines, theirsLines }\\n}\\n\\n/**\\n * Compute the full merge plan for (mine, base, theirs): a list of parts in\\n * base order, each either a stable run of base lines or a resolved region.\\n */\\nfunction computeMergePlan(mine, base, theirs) {\\n  const mineMatches = computeMatches(mine, base)\\n  const It takes exactly one argument, `merge`, `merge2` or `regions`, and reads three documents from standard input. Split the input on newlines and drop the empty tail a trailing newline leaves; a line that is exactly `%%%` separates the documents, and every other line belongs to the document it sits in. The three documents, in order, are mine, the base they both derive from, and theirs. A document may hold no line at all. Exit 0 when the merge is clean and 1 when any conflict was written, on every mode.\n\nCompare each side against the base with a longest common subsequence over whole lines, comparing them as exact strings. When two subsequences are equally long, take the one whose matches come earliest: walking both documents from the start, take a match whenever the two current lines are equal, and otherwise advance the side whose remaining subsequence is at least as long as the other's, preferring to advance the base when they tie. Every stretch the subsequence does not cover is a change, described as a run of base lines replaced by a run of that side's lines; an insertion replaces zero base lines and a deletion inserts zero lines.\n\nGather the changes from both sides into one list ordered by where they start in the base, with the changes from mine before the changes from theirs when they start at the same place. The base lines before a region and after the last one form stable regions, which the merge keeps unchanged.\n\nA region only one change touches resolves to that side's lines. For a region several changes touch, each side's replacement runs from the smallest start of its own changes, moved back by however far the region begins before those changes do in the base, to the largest end of its own changes, moved forward by however far the region ends after them. It reads a build graph from standard input and takes one of three argument forms: `waves`, `explain`, or `pack` followed by a worker count. Split the input on newlines and drop the empty tail a trailing newline leaves. Trim each line; ignore it when it is empty or begins with `#`; otherwise split it on runs of whitespace.\n\nA `task <name> <cost>` line declares a task. A name is one or more of the characters `A` to `Z`, `a` to `z`, `0` to `9`, `_`, `.` and `-`. A cost is an unsigned decimal integer written without a sign and without separators, so `01` is the cost 1. A `dep <name> <dependency>...` line makes the first name depend on every later name on the line; a repeated edge counts once, and a `dep` line may appear before the tasks it names. Every name a `dep` line uses must be declared by some `task` line somewhere in the input. An empty input is a valid graph with no task.\n\n`waves` prints one line per wave, `wave K: ` followed by the wave's task names in ascending order separated by single spaces, and then a final line `total T`. Wave 0 holds every task with no dependency and wave K holds every task whose deepest dependency is in wave K-1. `T` is the makespan with unlimited workers: the greatest finish time, or 0 when there is no task. A task's start is the greatest finish of its dependencies, or 0 when it has none, and its finish is its start plus its cost.\n\n`explain` prints one line per task in ascending name order, `<name> start=<s> finish=<f> depth=<d>`, where the start and finish are the ones above and the depth is the wave the task sits in, and then the same final `total T` line.\n\n`pack <workers>` simulates the same graph on that many workers. Time starts at 0. While a worker is free at the current instant and some task is ready, meaning every one of its dependencies has already finished at or before that instant, take the ready task with the greatest priority, breaking ties by ascending name, and start it on the free worker with the smallest index. A task's priority is the cost of the longest chain that begins at it, counting its own cost and then the costs down to any task that nothing depends on. A task of cost 0 finishes at the instant it starts, so its worker is free again at once and its dependents may start in the same instant. When nothing more can start, time advances to the next instant at which a worker becomes free, and the loop repeats. The output is one line per worker in index order, `worker <i>:` followed by ` <name>@<start>-<finish>` for each task it ran in the order it ran them, and then a final line `makespan M` holding the greatest finish, or 0 when there is no task.\n\nA cycle makes the graph unschedulable: write `error: cycle: ` followed by the cycle's names joined with ` -> ` and ending with a repeat of the first name, then exit 3, having written nothing to standard output. The reported cycle is the first one a depth-first search finds when it starts from each task in ascending name order and walks each task's dependencies in ascending name order; it runs from the task the search re-entered, through the search stack, back to that task.\n\nEvery other failure writes one line to standard error and exits 2, having written nothing to standard output; stop at the first one. The messages are `error: usage: cli.js waves|explain|pack <workers>` when the arguments are not one of the three forms; `error: workers must be a positive integer` when the worker count is not a decimal integer of at least 1 written without a sign or a leading zero; `error: line N: expected task <name> <cost>` and `error: line N: expected dep <name> <dependency>...` when a directive has the wrong number of words; `error: line N: invalid task name <name>` for a name outside the allowed characters; `error: line N: cost must be a non-negative integer`; `error: line N: task <name> is declared twice`; `error: line N: unknown directive <word>`; and `error: unknown task <name>`, with no line number, for the first name in input order that a `dep` line uses and no `task` line declares. Change only files under src/. Do not modify anything under test/ or package.json, and add no dependencies: node_modules must not exist. The standard is `node --test test/*.test.js` passing in the workspace root. dependency declarations. Throws a CliError for any malformed input,\\n * including a reference to a task nobody declared.\\n */\\nexport function parseGraph(text) {\\n  const lines = text.split('\\\\n')\\n  if (lines.length > 0 && lines[lines.length - 1] === '') {\\n    lines.pop()\\n  }\\n\\n  const tasks = new Map() // name -> cost\\n  const depDecls = [] // { name, deps, line }\\n\\n  for (let i = 0; i < lines.length; i++) {\\n    const lineNo = i + 1\\n    const trimmed = lines[i].trim()\\n    if (trimmed === '' || trimmed.startsWith('#')) continue\\n\\n    const words = trimmed.split(/\\\\s+/)\\n    const directive = words[0]\\n\\n    if (directive === 'task') {\\n      if (words.length !== 3) {\\n        throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: expected task <name> <cost>`)\\n      }\\n      const [, name, costStr] = words\\n      if (!NAME_RE.test(name)) {\\n        throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: invalid task name ${name}`)\\n      }\\n      if (!COST_RE.test(costStr)) {\\n        throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: cost must be a non-negative integer`)\\n      }\\n      if (tasks.has(name)) {\\n        throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: task ${name} is declared twice`)\\n      }\\n      tasks.set(name, parseInt(costStr, 10))\\n    } else if (directive === 'dep') {\\n      if (words.length < 3) {\\n        throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: expected dep <name> <dependency>...`)\\n      }\\n      const name = words[1]\\n      const deps = words.slice(2)\\n      if (!NAME_RE.test(name)) {\\n        throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: invalid task name ${name}`)\\n      }\\n      for (const dep of deps) {\\n        if (!NAME_RE.test(dep)) {\\n          throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: invalid task name ${dep}`)\\n        }\\n      }\\n      depDecls.push({ name, deps, line: lineNo })\\n    } else {\\n      throw new CliError(`line ${lineNo}: unknown directive ${directive}`)\\n    }\\n  }\\n\\n  // Every name a dep line uses must be declared. It reads a build graph from standard input and takes one of three argument forms: `waves`, `explain`, or `pack` followed by a worker count. Split the input on newlines and drop the empty tail a trailing newline leaves. Trim each line; ignore it when it is empty or begins with `#`; otherwise split it on runs of whitespace.\n\nA `task <name> <cost>` line declares a task. A name is one or more of the characters `A` to `Z`, `a` to `z`, `0` to `9`, `_`, `.` and `-`. A cost is an unsigned decimal integer written without a sign and without separators, so `01` is the cost 1. A `dep <name> <dependency>...` line makes the first name depend on every later name on the line; a repeated edge counts once, and a `dep` line may appear before the tasks it names. Every name a `dep` line uses must be declared by some `task` line somewhere in the input. An empty input is a valid graph with no task.\n\n`waves` prints one line per wave, `wave K: ` followed by the wave's task names in ascending order separated by single spaces, and then a final line `total T`. Wave 0 holds every task with no dependency and wave K holds every task whose deepest dependency is in wave K-1. `T` is the makespan with unlimited workers: the greatest finish time, or 0 when there is no task. A task's start is the greatest finish of its dependencies, or 0 when it has none, and its finish is its start plus its cost.\n\n`explain` prints one line per task in ascending name order, `<name> start=<s> finish=<f> depth=<d>`, where the start and finish are the ones above and the depth is the wave the task sits in, and then the same final `total T` line.\n\n`pack <workers>` simulates the same graph on that many workers. Time starts at 0. While a worker is free at the current instant and some task is ready, meaning every one of its dependencies has already finished at or before that instant, take the ready task with the greatest priority, breaking ties by ascending name, and start it on the free worker with the smallest index. A task's priority is the cost of the longest chain that begins at it, counting its own cost and then the costs down to any task that nothing depends on. A task of cost 0 finishes at the instant it starts, so its worker is free again at once and its dependents may start in the same instant. When nothing more can start, time advances to the next instant at which a worker becomes free, and the loop repeats. The output is one line per worker in index order, `worker <i>:` followed by ` <name>@<start>-<finish>` for each task it ran in the order it ran them, and then a final line `makespan M` holding the greatest finish, or 0 when there is no task.\n\nA cycle makes the graph unschedulable: write `error: cycle: ` followed by the cycle's names joined with ` -> ` and ending with a repeat of the first name, then exit 3, having written nothing to standard output. The reported cycle is the first one a depth-first search finds when it starts from each task in ascending name order and walks each task's dependencies in ascending name order; it runs from the task the search re-entered, through the search stack, back to that task.\n\nEvery other failure writes one line to standard error and exits 2, having written nothing to standard output; stop at the first one. It must appear exactly once, before any ballot, and must name at least one candidate; a name is one or more of the characters `A` to `Z`, `a` to `z`, `0` to `9`, `_` and `-`, and no name may repeat. The line `ballot <count> <candidate>...` records `count` identical ballots ranking those candidates from most to least preferred. The count is a decimal integer of at least 1 written without a sign and without a leading zero, a ballot names at least one candidate, every name must have been declared, and no ballot may name a candidate twice. A ballot need not rank every candidate.\n\nCount in rounds. Every candidate starts continuing. In each round every ballot counts for the first candidate on it that is still continuing; a ballot whose named candidates are all eliminated is exhausted and counts for nobody. Let the active total be the number of ballots that counted for someone. When the active total is 0 the election ends with no winner. When some candidate's count, doubled, is greater than the active total, that candidate wins and the election ends. Otherwise exactly one candidate is eliminated and another round is counted.\n\nThe candidate eliminated is the one with the fewest votes in this round. When several tie for fewest, compare them across the rounds already counted, this one included, from the first round onwards: at the first round where their counts are not all equal, keep only those with the fewest votes in that round and compare the survivors the same way from the first round again. When no round separates them, eliminate the one whose name sorts last by code point.\n\n`rounds` prints, for each round in order, `round K: ` followed by one `<name>=<votes>` for every continuing candidate, separated by single spaces, and then `exhausted=<n>`. Candidates are listed by descending vote count, and by ascending name where counts tie. A round that eliminated someone is followed by the line `eliminated <name>`. `tally` instead prints one line, `rounds N`, holding how many rounds were counted. Both modes end with `winner <name>`, or `no winner` when no candidate ever reached the majority.\n\nA failure writes one line to standard error and exits 2, having written nothing to standard output; stop at the first one. The messages are `error: usage: cli.js rounds|tally` when the argument is not exactly one of the two mode words; `error: line N: candidates must be declared once, before any ballot`; `error: line N: expected candidates <name>...`; `error: line N: invalid candidate name <name>`; `error: line N: candidate <name> is declared twice`; `error: line N: expected ballot <count> <candidate>...`; `error: line N: count must be a positive integer`; `error: line N: unknown candidate <name>`; `error: line N: candidate <name> is repeated`; `error: line N: unknown directive <word>`; and `error: no candidates`, with no line number, when the input declares none. Change only files under src/. Do not modify anything under test/ or package.json, and add no dependencies: node_modules must not exist. The standard is `node --test test/*.test.js` passing in the workspace root. This task is also judged on inputs you do not see: a validator runs your program on its own held-back cases and compares the exit code and output byte for byte. The visible tests cover the main paths only. The count is a decimal integer of at least 1 written without a sign and without a leading zero, a ballot names at least one candidate, every name must have been declared, and no ballot may name a candidate twice. A ballot need not rank every candidate.\n\nCount in rounds. Every candidate starts continuing. In each round every ballot counts for the first candidate on it that is still continuing; a ballot whose named candidates are all eliminated is exhausted and counts for nobody. Let the active total be the number of ballots that counted for someone. When the active total is 0 the election ends with no winner. When some candidate's count, doubled, is greater than the active total, that candidate wins and the election ends. Otherwise exactly one candidate is eliminated and another round is counted.\n\nThe candidate eliminated is the one with the fewest votes in this round. When several tie for fewest, compare them across the rounds already counted, this one included, from the first round onwards: at the first round where their counts are not all equal, keep only those with the fewest votes in that round and compare the survivors the same way from the first round again. When no round separates them, eliminate the one whose name sorts last by code point.\n\n`rounds` prints, for each round in order, `round K: ` followed by one `<name>=<votes>` for every continuing candidate, separated by single spaces, and then `exhausted=<n>`. Candidates are listed by descending vote count, and by ascending name where counts tie. A round that eliminated someone is followed by the line `eliminated <name>`. `tally` instead prints one line, `rounds N`, holding how many rounds were counted. Both modes end with `winner <name>`, or `no winner` when no candidate ever reached the majority.\n\nA failure writes one line to standard error and exits 2, having written nothing to standard output; stop at the first one. The messages are `error: usage: cli.js rounds|tally` when the argument is not exactly one of the two mode words; `error: line N: candidates must be declared once, before any ballot`; `error: line N: expected candidates <name>...`; `error: line N: invalid candidate name <name>`; `error: line N: candidate <name> is declared twice`; `error: line N: expected ballot <count> <candidate>...`; `error: line N: count must be a positive integer`; `error: line N: unknown candidate <name>`; `error: line N: candidate <name> is repeated`; `error: line N: unknown directive <word>`; and `error: no candidates`, with no line number, when the input declares none. It takes exactly one argument, `tokens` or `stats`, reads UTF-8 source text from standard input, and scans it. Exit 0 when the whole input scans.\n\nPositions count from 1. Reading a line feed moves to the next line and sets the column back to 1. Reading a tab moves the column forward to the next multiple of 4 plus 1, so a tab in column 1 lands on column 5 and a tab in column 6 lands on column 9. Every other code point moves the column forward by 1. A token's position is where its first code point sits.\n\nSpaces, tabs, carriage returns and line feeds separate tokens and produce none. `//` starts a comment that runs to the next line feed or to the end of the input. `/*` starts a block comment that ends at the matching `*/` and nests, so `/* a /* b */ c */` is one comment; a block comment that never closes is an error reported at the `/*` that opened the outermost one.\n\nAn identifier starts with a letter or `_` and continues with letters, digits and `_`; it is a `keyword` when it is exactly `let`, `if`, `else`, `while`, `return`, `true`, `false` or `null`, and an `ident` otherwise. A `.` only continues a number when a digit follows it, and an exponent marker only continues one when a digit, or a sign and then a digit, follows it. A `_` may sit between two digits of the same run and nowhere else. A number followed immediately by a letter, a digit or `_` that the rules above did not consume, and a prefix with no digit after it, are both errors, and the reported text is everything the scanner ran through, up to and including those trailing characters.\n\nA `string` is written between double quotes and carries the escapes `\\n`, `\\t`, `\\r`, `\\\\`, `\\\"`, `\\0`, `\\xHH` with exactly two hexadecimal digits, and `\\u{H...}` with at least one hexadecimal digit naming a code point of at most 10FFFF. Any other escape is an error reported at its backslash. A line feed inside a string is an error reported where the line feed sits, and a string the input ends before closing is an error reported at its opening quote.
